Main articles: [Human mating strategies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mating_strategies "Human mating strategies"), [Mate choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mate_choice "Mate choice"), [Mating preferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mating_preferences "Mating preferences"), [Sex differences in psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_differences_in_psychology "Sex differences in psychology"), and [Sexual selection in humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_selection_in_humans "Sexual selection in humans")
See also: [Bateman's principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bateman%27s_principle "Bateman's principle")
Given that sexual reproduction is the means by which genes are propagated into future generations, sexual selection plays a large role in human evolution. Human [mating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mating "Mating"), then, is of interest to evolutionary psychologists who aim to investigate evolved mechanisms for attracting and securing mates. Several lines of research have stemmed from this interest, such as studies of mate selection,[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBussBarnes1986559%E2%80%93570LiBaileyKenrickLinsenmeier2002947%E2%80%93955Wilson1981-84) mate poaching, mate retention, [mating preferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mating_preferences "Mating preferences"), and [conflict between the sexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_conflict "Sexual conflict").[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmittBuss2001894%E2%80%93917Buss1988291%E2%80%93317ShackelfordSchmittBuss2005447%E2%80%93458Buss2004-85)
In 1972, [Robert Trivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Trivers "Robert Trivers") published an influential paper on sex differences, which is now referred to as [parental investment theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parental_investment "Parental investment").[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrivers1972136%E2%80%93179-86) The size differences of [gametes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gametes "Gametes") ([anisogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anisogamy "Anisogamy")) are the fundamental, defining difference between males (small gametes – sperm) and females (large gametes – ova). Trivers noted that anisogamy typically results in different levels of parental investment between the sexes, with females initially investing more. Trivers proposed that this difference in parental investment leads to the [sexual selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_selection "Sexual selection") of different [reproductive strategies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mating_strategies "Human mating strategies") between the sexes and to [sexual conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_conflict "Sexual conflict"). For example, he suggested that the sex that invests less in offspring will generally compete for access to the higher-investing sex to increase their [inclusive fitness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclusive_fitness "Inclusive fitness"). Trivers posited that differential parental investment led to the evolution of sexual dimorphisms in [mate choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mate_choice "Mate choice"), intra- and inter- sexual reproductive competition, and [courtship displays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtship_display "Courtship display"). In mammals, including humans, females make a much larger parental investment than males (i.e., [gestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestation "Gestation") followed by childbirth and [lactation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation "Lactation")). Parental investment theory is a branch of [life history theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_history_theory "Life history theory"). 
[Buss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Buss "David Buss") and [Schmitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_P._Schmitt "David P. Schmitt")'s 1993 [sexual strategies theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_strategies_theory "Sexual strategies theory") proposed that, due to differential parental investment, humans have evolved sexually dimorphic adaptations related to _"sexual accessibility, fertility assessment, commitment seeking and avoidance, immediate and enduring resource procurement, paternity certainty, assessment of mate value, and parental investment."_ Their strategic interference theory suggested that conflict between the sexes occurs when the preferred reproductive strategies of one sex interfere with those of the other sex, resulting in the activation of emotional responses such as anger or jealousy[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBussSchmitt1993204%E2%80%93232-87)
Women are generally more selective when choosing mates, especially under long-term mating conditions. However, under some circumstances, engaging in multiple sexual relationships can provide benefits to women as well, such as fertility insurance, trading up to better genes, reducing the risk of inbreeding, and insurance protection of her offspring.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-88)
Due to male paternity uncertainty, sex differences have been found in the domains of [sexual jealousy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_jealousy "Sexual jealousy").[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuss1989735%E2%80%93747BussLarsenWestenSemmelroth1992251%E2%80%93255-89) Females generally react more adversely to emotional infidelity, and males will react more to sexual infidelity. This pattern is predicted because mating costs differ between the sexes. Women, on average, should prefer a mate who can offer resources (e.g., financial, commitment); thus, a woman risks losing such resources when a mate commits emotional infidelity. Men, on the other hand, are never certain of the genetic paternity of their children because they do not bear the offspring themselves. This suggests that for men, sexual infidelity would generally be more aversive than emotional infidelity because investing resources in another man's offspring does not lead to the propagation of their own genes.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalat2012-90)
Another interesting line of research examines women's mate preferences across the [ovulatory cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovulation "Ovulation").[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaseltonMiller200650%E2%80%9373GangestadSimpsonCousinsGarver-Apgar2004203%E2%80%93207-91) The theoretical underpinning of this research is that ancestral women would have evolved mechanisms to select mates with certain traits depending on their hormonal status. Known as the [ovulatory shift hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovulatory_shift_hypothesis "Ovulatory shift hypothesis"), the theory posits that, during the ovulatory phase of a woman's cycle (approximately days 10–15 of a woman's cycle),[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilcoxDunsonWeinbergTrussell2001211%E2%80%93215-92) a woman who mated with a male with high genetic quality would have been more likely, on average, to produce and bear a healthy offspring than a woman who mated with a male with low genetic quality. These putative preferences are expected to be especially apparent in short-term mating domains because a potential male mate would offer only genes to a potential offspring. This hypothesis allows researchers to examine whether women select mates with characteristics indicative of high genetic quality during the high fertility phase of their ovulatory cycles. Indeed, studies have shown that women's preferences vary across the ovulatory cycle. In particular, Haselton and Miller (2006) showed that highly fertile women prefer creative but poor men as short-term mates. Creativity may be a proxy for good genes.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2001-93) Research by Steven Gangestad in 2004 indicates that highly fertile women prefer men who display social presence and intrasexual competition; these traits may act as cues that help women predict which men may have, or be able to acquire, resources.[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGangestadSimpsonCousinsGarver-Apgar2004203%E2%80%93207-94)
### Parenting
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Parenting")]
Main article: [Evolutionary psychology of parenting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology_of_parenting "Evolutionary psychology of parenting")
Reproduction inherently involves a cost for women, and men may also bear this cost. Individuals have limited time and resources to dedicate to their children, and this commitment can harm their own future health, survival, and reproductive potential. Parental investment is any parental expenditure (time, energy, etc.) that benefits one [offspring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offspring "Offspring") at a cost to parents' ability to invest in other components of fitness. [[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClutton-Brock1991Trivers197135%E2%80%9357-95) Components of fitness (Beatty 1992Components of fitness (Beatty 1992) include the well-being of existing offspring, parents' future [reproduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_reproduction "Sexual reproduction"), and inclusive fitness through aid to kin ([Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._D._Hamilton "W. D. Hamilton"), 1964). Parental investment theory is a branch of life history theory.[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-96)
The benefits of parental investment to the offspring are large and are associated with the effects on condition, growth, survival, and ultimately, on the reproductive success of the offspring. However, these benefits can come at the cost of the parent's future reproductive ability, e.g., increased risk of injury when defending offspring against predators, the loss of mating opportunities whilst rearing offspring, and an increase in the time to the next reproduction. Overall, parents are [selected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selection_\(biology\) "Selection \(biology\)") to maximize the difference between the benefits and the costs, and parental care will likely evolve when the benefits exceed the costs. 
The [Cinderella effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella_effect "Cinderella effect") is an alleged high incidence of stepchildren being physically, emotionally, or sexually abused, neglected, murdered, or otherwise mistreated at the hands of their stepparents at significantly higher rates than their genetic counterparts. It takes its name from the fairy tale character Cinderella, who in the story was cruelly mistreated by her stepmother and stepsisters.[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalyWilson1999-97) Martin Daly and Margo Wilson noted in their 1999 book:
> _Evolutionary thinking led to the discovery of the most important risk factor for child homicide – the presence of a stepparent. Parental efforts and investments are valuable resources, and selection favors those parental psyches that allocate effort effectively to promote fitness. The adaptive problems that challenge parental decision-making include accurately identifying one's offspring, allocating resources among them with sensitivity to their needs and abilities, and converting parental investment into fitness increments.... Stepchildren were seldom or never so valuable to one's expected fitness as one's own offspring would be, and those parental psyches that were easily parasitized by just any appealing youngster must always have incurred a selective disadvantage._
— Martin Daly and Margo Wilson, The Truth about Cinderella: A Darwinian View of Parental Love, [[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalyWilson1999-97)
However, they note that not all stepparents will "want" to abuse their partner's children, or that genetic parenthood is any insurance against abuse. They see step-parental care as primarily "mating effort" towards the genetic parent. 
### Family and kin
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Family and kin")]
See also: [Human inclusive fitness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_inclusive_fitness "Human inclusive fitness") and [Kin selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kin_selection "Kin selection")
Inclusive fitness is the sum of an organism's classical fitness (how many of its own offspring it produces and supports) and the number of equivalents of its own offspring it can add to the population by supporting others. The first component is called classical fitness by Hamilton (1964).[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-98)
From the gene's point of view, evolutionary success ultimately depends on leaving behind the maximum number of copies of itself in the population. Until 1964, it was generally believed that genes only achieved this by causing the individual to leave the maximum number of viable offspring. However, in 1964, W. D. Hamilton proved mathematically that, because close relatives of an organism share some identical genes, a gene can also increase its evolutionary success by promoting the reproduction and survival of these related or otherwise similar individuals. Hamilton concluded that this leads natural selection to favor organisms that would behave in ways that maximize their inclusive fitness. It is also true that natural selection favors behavior that maximizes personal fitness. 
Hamilton's rule describes mathematically whether or not a gene for altruistic behavior will spread in a population:      **_rb > c_**
where 
  * **c** is the reproductive cost to the altruist,
  * **b** is the reproductive benefit to the recipient of the altruistic behavior, and
  * **r** is the probability, above the population average, of the individuals sharing an altruistic gene – commonly viewed as "degree of relatedness".


The concept explains how natural selection can perpetuate altruism. If there is an "altruism gene" (or complex of genes) that influences an organism's behavior to be helpful and protective of relatives and their offspring, this behavior also increases the proportion of the altruism gene in the population, because relatives are likely to share genes with the altruist due to [common descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identical_by_descent "Identical by descent"). Altruists may also recognize altruistic behavior in unrelated individuals and be inclined to support them. As Dawkins points out in _The Selfish Gene_ (Chapter 6) and _The Extended Phenotype,_ this must be distinguished from the [green-beard effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green-beard_effect "Green-beard effect").[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawkins1989Chapter_6Dawkins1999-99)
Although it is generally true that humans tend to be more altruistic toward their kin than toward non-kin, the relevant proximate mechanisms that mediate this cooperation have been debated (see [kin recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kin_recognition "Kin recognition")), with some arguing that kin status is determined primarily via social and cultural factors (such as co-residence, maternal association of sibs, etc.), while others have argued that kin recognition can also be mediated by biological factors such as facial resemblance and immunogenetic similarity of the major histocompatibility complex (MHC). [[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-100)
The interaction between these social and biological kin-recognition factors was discussed in the 2007 [Nature (journal)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_\(journal\) "Nature \(journal\)") article - _The architecture of human kin detection._[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-101)
Whatever the proximate mechanisms of kin recognition, there is substantial evidence that humans are generally more altruistic towards close genetic kin compared to genetic non-kin.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBussHaseltonShackelfordBleske1998533%E2%80%93548GaulinMcBurney2004WorkmanReader2008-102)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Interactions with non-kin / reciprocity")]
Although interactions with non-kin are generally less altruistic compared to those with kin, cooperation can be maintained with non-kin via mutually beneficial reciprocity as was proposed by Robert Trivers[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrivers197135%E2%80%9357-20) If there are repeated encounters between the same two players in an evolutionary game in which each of them can choose either to "cooperate" or "defect", then a strategy of mutual cooperation may be favored even if it pays each player, in the short term, to defect when the other cooperates. Direct reciprocity can lead to the evolution of cooperation only if the probability, w, of another encounter between the same two individuals exceeds the cost-to-benefit ratio of the altruistic act:      _w > c/b_
Reciprocity can also be indirect if information about previous interactions is shared. Reputation enables the evolution of cooperation by indirect reciprocity. Natural selection favors strategies that base the decision to help on the recipient's reputation: studies show that people who are more helpful are more likely to receive help. The calculations of indirect reciprocity are complicated, and only a tiny fraction of this universe has been uncovered, but again, a simple rule has emerged.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-103) Indirect reciprocity can only promote cooperation if the probability, q, of knowing someone's reputation exceeds the cost-to-benefit ratio of the altruistic act:      _q > c/b_
One important problem with this explanation is that individuals may be able to evolve the capacity to obscure their reputations, reducing the probability, q, that it will be known.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-104)
Trivers argues that friendship and various social emotions evolved in order to manage reciprocity. Liking and disliking, he says, evolved to help present-day humans' ancestors form coalitions with others who reciprocated and to exclude those who did not reciprocate. Moral indignation may have evolved to prevent one's altruism from being exploited by cheaters, and gratitude may have motivated present-day humans' ancestors to reciprocate appropriately after benefiting from others' altruism. Likewise, present-day humans feel guilty when they fail to reciprocate. These social motivations match what evolutionary psychologists expect to see in adaptations that evolved to maximize the benefits and minimize the drawbacks of reciprocity.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaulinMcBurney2004323%E2%80%93352-105)
According to evolutionary psychologists, humans possess psychological adaptations that developed to help us spot cheaters, also known as non-reciprocators. In 1993, Robert Frank and his associates found that participants in a prisoner's dilemma scenario were often able to predict whether their partners would "cheat", based on a half-hour of unstructured social interaction. In a 1996 experiment, for example, [Linda Mealey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Mealey "Linda Mealey") and her colleagues found that people were better at remembering faces when those faces were associated with stories about those individuals cheating (such as embezzling money from a church).[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaulinMcBurney2004323%E2%80%93352-105)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: Strong reciprocity \(or "tribal reciprocity"\)")]
Main article: [Strong reciprocity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strong_reciprocity "Strong reciprocity")
Humans may have an evolved set of psychological adaptations that predispose them to be more cooperative than otherwise would be expected with members of their tribal [in-group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingroups_and_outgroups "Ingroups and outgroups"), and nastier to members of tribal [out-groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingroups_and_outgroups "Ingroups and outgroups"). These adaptations may have been a consequence of tribal warfare.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-106)
Humans may also have predispositions for "[altruistic punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third-party_punishment "Third-party punishment")" – to punish in-group members who violate in-group rules, even when this altruistic behavior cannot be justified in terms of helping those you are related to ([kin selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kin_selection "Kin selection")), cooperating with those who you will interact with again ([direct reciprocity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocity_\(evolution\)#Direct_reciprocity "Reciprocity \(evolution\)")), or cooperating to better your reputation with others ([indirect reciprocity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indirect_reciprocity "Indirect reciprocity")).[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGintis2000169%E2%80%93179HenrichChudek201229%E2%80%9330-107)
## Evolutionary psychology and culture
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Evolutionary psychology and culture")]
Main article: [Evolutionary psychology and culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology_and_culture "Evolutionary psychology and culture")
Though evolutionary psychology has traditionally focused on individual-level behaviors, determined by species-typical psychological adaptations, considerable work has examined how these adaptations shape and, ultimately, govern culture. In 1989, Tooby and Cosmides argued that the mind consists of many domain-specific psychological adaptations, some of which may constrain what cultural material is learned or taught. As opposed to a domain-general cultural acquisition program, where an individual passively receives culturally-transmitted material from the group, 
Tooby and Cosmides, among others, argue that:
> _The psyche evolved to generate adaptive rather than repetitive behavior, and hence critically analyzes the behavior of those surrounding it in highly structured and patterned ways, to be used as a rich (but by no means the only) source of information out of which to construct a 'private culture' or individually tailored adaptive system; in consequence, this system may or may not mirror the behavior of others in any given respect._  
>  Tooby and Cosmides 1989.[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-ReferenceB-108)
Biological explanations of human culture also brought criticism to evolutionary psychology: Evolutionary psychologists see the human psyche and physiology as a genetic product and assume that genes contain the information for the development and control of the organism and that this information is transmitted from one generation to the next via genes. Evolutionary psychologists, therefore, view the physical and psychological characteristics of humans as genetically programmed. Even then, when evolutionary psychologists acknowledge the influence of the environment on human development, they understand the environment only as an activator or trigger for the programmed developmental instructions encoded in genes.[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_RegenmortelHull2003Buller2005-109) Evolutionary psychologists, for example, believe that the human brain is composed of innate modules, each specialised for a specific task, e.g., an anxiety module. According to evolutionary psychologists, these modules are present before the organism actually develops and are then activated by some environmental event. Critics object that this view is reductionist and that cognitive specialisation arises only through the interaction of humans with their real environment, rather than the environment of distant ancestors. Interdisciplinary approaches are increasingly striving to mediate between these opposing points of view and to highlight that biological and cultural causes need not be antithetical to explain human behaviour, including complex cultural achievements.[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_RegenmortelHull2003Buller2005134%E2%80%93135Seethaler201574%E2%80%9382-110)
## In psychology sub-fields
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: In psychology sub-fields")]
### Developmental psychology
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Developmental psychology")]
Main article: [Evolutionary developmental psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_developmental_psychology "Evolutionary developmental psychology")
According to [Paul Baltes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Baltes "Paul Baltes"), the benefits granted by evolutionary selection decrease with age. Natural selection has not eliminated many harmful conditions and nonadaptive characteristics that appear among older adults, such as [Alzheimer's disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alzheimer%27s_disease "Alzheimer's disease"). If it were a disease that killed 20-year-olds instead of 70-year-olds, this might have been a disease that natural selection could have eliminated ages ago. Thus, unaided by evolutionary pressures against non-adaptive conditions, modern humans suffer the aches, pains, and infirmities of aging, and as the benefits of evolutionary selection decrease with age, the need for modern technological mediums against non-adaptive conditions increases.[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTESantrock2005-111)
### Social psychology
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Social psychology")]
As humans are a highly social species, there are many adaptive problems associated with navigating the social world (e.g., maintaining allies, managing status hierarchies, interacting with outgroup members, coordinating social activities, collective decision-making). Researchers in the emerging field of evolutionary social psychology have made many discoveries pertaining to topics traditionally studied by social psychologists, including person perception, social cognition, attitudes, altruism, emotions, [group dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_dynamics "Group dynamics"), [leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leadership "Leadership"), motivation, prejudice, intergroup relations, and cross-cultural differences.[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTENeubergKenrickSchaller2010761%E2%80%93796SchallerSimpsonKenrick2006LevineKameda2012Chapter_10Van_VugtSchaller20081%E2%80%936-112)
When endeavouring to solve a problem, humans, at an early age, show determination, whereas chimpanzees do not exhibit a comparable facial expression. Researchers suspect the human-determined expression evolved because when a human is determinedly working on a problem, other people will frequently help.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-113)
### Abnormal psychology
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Abnormal psychology")]
Main article: [Evolutionary psychiatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychiatry "Evolutionary psychiatry")
Adaptationist hypotheses regarding the etiology of psychological disorders are often based on analogies between physiological and psychological dysfunctions, as noted in the table below. Prominent theorists and evolutionary psychiatrists include [Michael T. McGuire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_T._McGuire "Michael T. McGuire"), [Anthony Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Stevens_\(Jungian_analyst\) "Anthony Stevens \(Jungian analyst\)"), and [Randolph M. Nesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randolph_M._Nesse "Randolph M. Nesse"). They, and others, suggest that mental disorders are due to the interactive effects of both nature and nurture, and often have multiple contributing causes.[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTENesseWilliams1996GaulinMcBurney20041%E2%80%9324-114)
Possible causes of psychological abnormalities from an adaptationist perspective   
Summary based on information contained in these textbooks[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBussHaseltonShackelfordBleske1998533%E2%80%93548GaulinMcBurney2004WorkmanReader2008CosmidesTooby1999453%E2%80%93464-115) Causal mechanism of failure or malfunction of adaptation | Physiological Example | Hypothesized Psychological Example   
---|---|---  
**Functioning adaptation (adaptive defense)** | Fever / Vomiting   
(functional responses to infection or ingestion of toxins) | Mild depression or anxiety (functional responses to mild loss or stress/ reduction of social interactions to prevent infection by contagious pathogens)[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-ncbi.nlm.nih.gov-116)[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-117)  
**By-product of an adaptation(s)** | Intestinal gas   
(byproduct of digestion of fiber) |  [Sexual fetishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_fetishism "Sexual fetishism") (?)  
(possible byproduct of normal sexual arousal adaptations that have 'imprinted' on unusual objects or situations)  
**Adaptations with multiple effects** |  [Sickle cell disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sickle_cell_disease "Sickle cell disease") (Gene that imparts malaria resistance, in homozygous form, causes sickle cell anemia) |  [Schizophrenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schizophrenia "Schizophrenia") or [bipolar disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipolar_disorder "Bipolar disorder") (May be side-effects of adaptations for high levels of creativity, perhaps dependent on alternate developmental trajectories)  
**Malfunctioning adaptation** | Allergies   
(over-reactive immunological responses) |  [Autism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autism "Autism")   
(possible malfunctioning of [theory of mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_mind "Theory of mind") module)  
**[Frequency-dependent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frequency-dependent_selection "Frequency-dependent selection") morphs** | The two sexes / Different blood and immune system types  |  [Personality disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personality_disorders "Personality disorders")   
(may represent alternative behavioral strategies possibly dependent on its prevalence in the population)  
**Mismatch between ancestral & current environments** |  [Type 2 Diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_2_Diabetes "Type 2 Diabetes")   
(May be related to the abundance of sugary foods in the modern world) | More frequent modern interaction with strangers (compared to family and close friends) may predispose greater incidence of depression & anxiety   
**Tails of[normal distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_distribution "Normal distribution") (bell curve)** |  [Dwarfism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarfism "Dwarfism") or [gigantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigantism "Gigantism") | Extremities of the distribution of cognitive and personality traits  
(e.g., extremely introversion and extraversion, or [intellectual giftedness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellectual_giftedness "Intellectual giftedness") and [intellectual disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellectual_disability "Intellectual disability"))  
Evolutionary psychologists have suggested that schizophrenia and bipolar disorder may reflect a side-effect of genes with fitness benefits, such as increased creativity.[[d]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-118) A 1994 report by the American Psychiatry Association found that the incidence of schizophrenia occurs at roughly the same rate in Western and non-Western cultures, and in industrialized and pastoral societies, suggesting that schizophrenia is not a disease of civilization nor an arbitrary social invention.[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaulinMcBurney2004239%E2%80%93256-119) Sociopathy may represent an evolutionarily stable strategy, by which a small number of people who cheat on social contracts benefit in a society consisting mostly of non-sociopaths[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaulinMcBurney20041%E2%80%9324-15) Mild depression may be an adaptive response to withdraw from, and re-evaluate, situations that have led to disadvantageous outcomes (the "analytical rumination hypothesis")[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-ncbi.nlm.nih.gov-116) (see [Evolutionary approaches to depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_approaches_to_depression "Evolutionary approaches to depression")). 
Trofimova reviewed the most consistent psychological and behavioural sex differences in psychological abilities and disabilities and linked them to Geodakyan's _Evolutionary Theory of Sex (ETS)._[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-Trofimova-120) She pointed out that a pattern of consistent sex differences in physical, verbal, and social disabilities corresponds to the idea of the ETS, considering sex dimorphism as a functional specialization of a species. 
Sex differentiation, according to the ETS, creates two partitions within a species:      
  * conservational (females)
  * variational (males).


In females, superiority in verbal abilities, higher rule obedience, socialisation, empathy, and agreeableness can be seen as reflecting the systemic conservation function of the female sex. Men often excel in areas such as exploration, risk-taking, spatial abilities, physical strength, and aggression. This pattern, along with elevated birth and accidental death rates, could indicate the male sex's systematic variational function, which explores the limits of advantageous traits. This suggests that psychological sex differences are affected by a species' general drive to expand its adaptive flexibility while retaining valuable characteristics. 
Moreover, Trofimova suggested a "redundancy pruning" hypothesis as an upgrade of the ETS theory. She pointed out higher rates of psychopathy, dyslexia, autism, and schizophrenia in males, in comparison to females. She suggested that the variational function of the "male partition" might also provide irrelevance/redundancy pruning of excesses in a bank of beneficial characteristics of a species, while continuing resistance to any changes from the norm-driven conservational partition of species. This might explain a contradictory allocation of a high drive for social status/power in the male sex, with their lower (among the two sexes) abilities for social interaction. The high rates of communicative disorders and psychopathy in males might facilitate their higher rates of disengagement from normative expectations and their insensitivity to social disapproval when they deliberately do not follow social norms.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-Trofimova-120)
Some of these speculations have yet to be developed into fully testable hypotheses, and much research is required to confirm their validity.[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connell200437Sims2001558%E2%80%93559-121)
### Antisocial and criminal behavior
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: Antisocial and criminal behavior")]
Main article: [Biosocial criminology § Evolutionary psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosocial_criminology#Evolutionary_psychology "Biosocial criminology")
Evolutionary psychology has been applied to explain [criminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal "Criminal") or otherwise immoral behavior as being adaptive or related to adaptive behaviors. Males are generally more aggressive than females, who are more selective of their partners because of the far greater effort they have to contribute to pregnancy and child-rearing. Males' greater aggressiveness is hypothesized to stem from the more intense reproductive competition they faced. Males of low status may be especially vulnerable to being childless. It may have been evolutionarily advantageous to engage in highly risky, violent behavior to increase their status and therefore reproductive success. This may explain why males are generally involved in more crimes, and why low status and being unmarried are associated with criminality. Furthermore, competition over females is argued to have been particularly intensive in late adolescence and young adulthood, which is theorized to explain why crime rates are particularly high during this period.[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFigueredoGladdenHohman2012201%E2%80%93221-122) Some sociologists have underlined differential exposure to androgens as the cause of these behaviors, notably Lee Ellis in his [evolutionary neuroandrogenic (ENA) theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_neuroandrogenic_theory "Evolutionary neuroandrogenic theory").[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-123)
Many conflicts that result in harm and death involve status, reputation, and seemingly trivial insults.[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFigueredoGladdenHohman2012201%E2%80%93221-122) [Steven Pinker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Pinker "Steven Pinker") in his book _[The Better Angels of Our Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Better_Angels_of_Our_Nature "The Better Angels of Our Nature")_ argues that in non-state societies without a police it was very important to have a credible [deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deterrence_theory "Deterrence theory") against aggression. Therefore, it was important to be perceived as having a credible reputation for retaliation, leading humans in developing instincts for [revenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenge "Revenge") as well as for protecting reputation ("[honor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor "Honor")"). Pinker argues that the development of the state and the police has dramatically reduced the level of violence compared to the ancestral environment. Whenever the state breaks down, which can be very local, such as in poor areas of a city, humans again organize in groups for protection and aggression, and concepts such as violent revenge and protecting honor again become extremely important.[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFigueredoGladdenHohman2012201%E2%80%93221-122)
Rape is theorized to be a reproductive strategy that facilitates the propagation of the rapist's progeny. Such a strategy may be adopted by men who otherwise are unlikely to be appealing to women and therefore cannot form legitimate relationships, or by high-status men on socially vulnerable women who are unlikely to retaliate to increase their reproductive success even further.[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-124) The [sociobiological theories of rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociobiological_theories_of_rape "Sociobiological theories of rape") are highly controversial, as traditional theories typically do not consider rape to be a behavioral adaptation, and objections to this theory are made on ethical, religious, political, as well as scientific grounds. 
### Psychology of religion
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: Psychology of religion")]
Main article: [Evolutionary psychology of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology_of_religion "Evolutionary psychology of religion")
Adaptationist perspectives on [religious belief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_belief "Religious belief") suggest that, like all behavior, religious beliefs are a product of the human brain. As with all other organ functions, [cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition "Cognition")'s functional structure has been argued to have a genetic foundation and is therefore subject to the effects of natural selection and sexual selection. Like other organs and tissues, this functional structure should be universally shared amongst humans and should have solved important problems of survival and reproduction in ancestral environments. However, evolutionary psychologists remain divided on whether religious belief is more likely a consequence of evolved psychological adaptations or a byproduct of other cognitive adaptations.[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTESosisAlcorta2003264%E2%80%93274LienardBoyer2006824%E2%80%93827SzocikEyghen202149%E2%80%9381-125)
### Coalitional psychology
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: Coalitional psychology")]
Coalitional psychology is an approach to explaining political behaviors among different [coalitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalitions "Coalitions") and the [conditionality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conditionality "Conditionality") of these behaviors in an evolutionary psychological perspective. This approach assumes that since human beings appeared on the earth, they have evolved to live in groups instead of living as individuals to achieve benefits such as more mating opportunities and increased status.[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTELopezMcDermottPetersen201148%E2%80%9383-126) Human beings thus naturally think and act in a way that manages and negotiates [group dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_dynamics "Group dynamics"). 
Coalitional psychology offers falsifiable [ex ante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex-ante "Ex-ante") prediction by positing five hypotheses on how these [psychological adaptations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_adaptation "Psychological adaptation") operate:[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTELopezMcDermottPetersen201166%E2%80%9382-127)
  * Humans represent groups as a special category of individual, unstable, and with a short shadow of the future
  * [Political entrepreneurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_entrepreneurs "Political entrepreneurs") strategically manipulate the coalitional environment, often appealing to emotional devices such as "outrage" to inspire [collective action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_action "Collective action").
  * [Relative gains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_gains "Relative gains") dominate relations with enemies, whereas [absolute gains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_gains "Absolute gains") characterize relations with allies.
  * Coalitional size and male physical strength will positively predict individual support for aggressive foreign policies.
  * Individuals with children, particularly women, will vary in adopting aggressive foreign policies than those without progeny.


## Reception and criticism
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Reception and criticism")]
Main article: [Criticism of evolutionary psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_evolutionary_psychology "Criticism of evolutionary psychology")
Critics of evolutionary psychology accuse it of promoting genetic determinism, [pan-adaptationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-adaptationism "Pan-adaptationism") (the idea that all behaviors and anatomical features are adaptations), unfalsifiable hypotheses, distal or ultimate explanations of behavior when proximate explanations are superior, and malevolent political or moral ideas.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-128)
### Ethical implications
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Ethical implications")]
Critics have argued that evolutionary psychology might be used to justify existing social hierarchies and [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary "Reactionary") policies. It has also been suggested by critics that evolutionary psychologists' theories and interpretations of empirical data rely heavily on [ideological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology "Ideology") assumptions about race and gender.[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoseRose20001%E2%80%9313WilsonDietrichClark2003669%E2%80%93681CaporaelBrewer19911%E2%80%939-129)
In response to such criticism, evolutionary psychologists often caution against committing the [naturalistic fallacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalistic_fallacy "Naturalistic fallacy") – the assumption that "what is natural" is necessarily a moral good.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilsonDietrichClark2003669%E2%80%93681Levy2004459%E2%80%93472Pinker2002-130)[_[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_] However, their caution against committing the naturalistic fallacy has been criticized as a means to stifle legitimate ethical discussions. [[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilsonDietrichClark2003669%E2%80%93681-131)
### Contradictions in models
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Contradictions in models")]
Some criticisms of evolutionary psychology point to contradictions between different aspects of adaptive scenarios posited by evolutionary psychology. One example is evolutionary psychology's claim that extended social groups select for modern human brains. However, the synaptic function of modern human brains requires high amounts of many specific [essential nutrients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrient#Essentiality "Nutrient"). Sharing this requirement among all individuals in a population would decrease the possibility of forming large groups, due to bottleneck foods with rare essential nutrients capping group sizes. Furthermore, it is noted[_[by whom?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch")_] that some insects have societies with distinct ranks for individuals, despite having smaller [connectomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connectome "Connectome") relative to humans. Also, monkeys remain socially functioning after the removal of most of their brains, which doesn't support the notion that big brains promote social networking. 
The model of males as both providers and protectors is criticized for the impossibility of being in two places at once; the male cannot both protect his family at home and be out hunting at the same time. In the case of the claim that a provider male could buy protection service for his family from other males by [bartering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barter "Barter") food that he had hunted, critics point at the fact that the most valuable food (the food that contained the rarest essential nutrients) would be different in different ecologies. As such, valuable foods may be primarily vegetable in some geographical areas and animal in others, making it impossible for hunting styles relying on physical strength or risk-taking to be universally of similar value. 
A contradiction also exists between evolutionary psychology's claim that men need to assess a mate's sexual viability more than women. Further, the claim that male sexual jealousy guards against infidelity is also criticized, as it would be pointless for a male to be fast to assess female fertility if he needed to assess the risk of there being a jealous male nearby. In that case, his chances of defeating him before mating would have to be assessed.[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrankenhuis20212376%E2%80%932377KenrickGriskeviciusMahmoud2016333%E2%80%93335-132)
## Standard social science model
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Standard social science model")]
Main article: [Standard social science model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_social_science_model "Standard social science model")
Evolutionary psychology has been entangled in the broader philosophical and social-science controversies surrounding [nature versus nurture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_versus_nurture "Nature versus nurture") debate. Evolutionary psychologists typically contrast evolutionary psychology with what they call the standard social science model (SSSM). They characterize the SSSM as the "[blank slate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blank_slate "Blank slate")", "[relativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factual_relativism "Factual relativism")", "[social constructionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_constructionism "Social constructionism")", and "[cultural determinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_determinism "Cultural determinism")" perspective that dominated the [social sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_science "Social science") throughout the 20th century and assumed that the mind was shaped almost entirely by culture.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPinker1999119%E2%80%93127-133) Critics have argued that evolutionary psychologists created a [false dichotomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_dilemma "False dilemma") between their own view and the [caricature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caricature "Caricature") of the SSSM. [[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichardson2007176Wallace2010136Solomon200417-134) Other critics regard the SSSM as a [rhetorical device](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetorical_device "Rhetorical device") or a [straw man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straw_man "Straw man")[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTELevy2004459%E2%80%93472Richardson2007176Sampson2009134%E2%80%93145-135) and suggest that the scientists whom evolutionary psychologists associate with the SSSM did not believe that the mind was a blank slate devoid of any natural predispositions.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTELevy2004459%E2%80%93472-136)
### Reductionism and determinism
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Reductionism and determinism")]
Some critics view evolutionary psychology as a form of genetic [reductionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reductionism#Reductionism_and_science "Reductionism") and [genetic determinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_determinism "Genetic determinism"), a common critique being that evolutionary psychology does not address the complexity of individual development and experience and fails to explain the influence of genes on behavior in individual cases.[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaiers2003426%E2%80%93435Plotkin2004149%E2%80%93150Beer1998-137) Evolutionary psychologists respond that they work within a nature-nurture interactionist framework that acknowledges that many psychological adaptations are facultative (sensitive to environmental variations during individual development). The discipline is generally not focused on proximate analyses of behavior; rather, its focues on the study of distal/ultimate causality (the evolution of psychological adaptations). The field of behavioral genetics focuses on the proximate influence of genes on behavior.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConferEastonFleischmanGoetz2010110%E2%80%93126-138)
### Testability of hypotheses
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Testability of hypotheses")]
See also: [Just-so story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just-so_story "Just-so story")
Evolutionary psychology's scientific credibility suffers from common criticisms that its hypotheses are untestable and arbitrary. For example, many current traits likely evolved for purposes other than their current ones.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchacterAddisBuckner2007657%E2%80%93661Ryle2005375%E2%80%93388-139) Therefore, since infinite explanations could exist for a trait's evolution, critics argue that pinpointing the precise reason is impossible. 
Although evolutionary psychology hypotheses are hard to verify, evolutionary psychologists maintain that verification is possible.[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy2003161%E2%80%93184-140)
> _Testing ideas about the evolutionary origins of psychological phenomena is indeed a challenging task, but not an impossible one._ [[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBussSchmittShackelfordKirkpatrick1998595%E2%80%93599Pinker1997-141)
Part of the critique of the scientific base of evolutionary psychology includes a critique of the concept of the Environment of Evolutionary Adaptation (EEA). Some critics have argued that researchers know so little about the environment in which _Homo sapiens_ evolved that explaining specific traits as an adaptation to that environment becomes highly speculative.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlotkin2004149%E2%80%93150-142) Evolutionary psychologists respond that they do know many things about this environment, including that present-day humans' ancestors were hunter-gatherers, and that they generally lived in small tribes.[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuss2005bChapter_1_-_Evolutionary_Psychology:_Foundations,_pp=5-67CosmidesTooby1992163%E2%80%93228-143)
Edward Hagen argues that the human past environments were not radically different in the same sense as the Carboniferous or Jurassic periods and that the animal and plant taxa of the era were similar to those of the modern world, as was the geology and ecology. Hagen argues that few would deny that other organs evolved in the EEA (for example, lungs evolving in an oxygen-rich atmosphere), yet critics question whether the brain's EEA is truly knowable, which he argues constitutes selective scepticism. Hagen also argues that most evolutionary psychology research rests on the fact that females can get pregnant and males cannot, which Hagen observes was also true in the EEA.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHagen20141%E2%80%9315Hagan2005145%E2%80%93173-144)
John Alcock describes this as the "No Time Machine Argument": critics argue that since it is not possible to travel back in time to the EEA, then it cannot be determined what was going on there, and thus what was adaptive. Alcock argues that present-day evidence allows researchers to be reasonably confident about the conditions of the EEA and that the fact that so many human behaviours are adaptive in the _current_ environment is evidence that the ancestral environment of humans had much in common with the present one, as these behaviours would have evolved in the ancestral environment. Thus, Alcock concludes that researchers can make predictions on the adaptive value of traits.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaryanskiMachalekTurner2015161%E2%80%93163-145) Similarly, Dominic Murphy argues that alternative explanations cannot merely just be forwarded but instead need their own evidence and predictions - if one explanation makes predictions that the others cannot, it is reasonable to have confidence in that explanation. In addition, Murphy argues that other historical sciences also make predictions about future phenomena to develop explanations about past phenomena. For example, cosmologists look for evidence of what we would expect to see in modern times if the Big Bang were true, while geologists make predictions about modern phenomena to determine if an asteroid wiped out the dinosaurs. Murphy argues that if other historical disciplines can conduct tests without a time machine, then the onus is on the critics to show why evolutionary psychology is untestable if other historical disciplines are not, as "methods should be judged across the board, not singled out for ridicule in one context."[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy2003161%E2%80%93184-140)
### Modularity of mind
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=42 "Edit section: Modularity of mind")]
Main article: [Modularity of mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modularity_of_mind "Modularity of mind")
Evolutionary psychologists generally presume that, like the body, the mind is composed of many evolved modular adaptations, although there is some disagreement within the discipline regarding the degree of general plasticity, or "generality," of some modules.[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzban2011ConferEastonFleischmanGoetz2010110%E2%80%93126-146) One suggestion for the evolution of modularity is that it offered greater fitness and reduced connection costs than non-modular networks.[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTECosmidesTooby1992163%E2%80%93228CluneMouretLipson2013-147)
In contrast, some academics argue that it is unnecessary to posit highly domain-specific modules and suggest that the brain's neural anatomy supports a model based on more domain-general faculties and processes.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPankseppPanksepp2000108%E2%80%93131BullerHardcastle2005127%E2%80%93201-148) Moreover, empirical support for the domain-specific theory stems almost entirely from performance on variations of the [Wason selection task](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wason_selection_task "Wason selection task"), which is extremely limited in scope as it tests only one subtype of deductive reasoning.[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaviesFetzerFoster19951%E2%80%9337O'BrienManfrinati201039%E2%80%9354-149)
### Cultural rather than genetic development of cognitive tools
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Cultural rather than genetic development of cognitive tools")]
Psychologist [Cecilia Heyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cecilia_Heyes "Cecilia Heyes") has argued that the picture presented by some evolutionary psychology of the human mind as a collection of cognitive instincts – organs of thought shaped by genetic evolution over very long time periods [[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-150)[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorin2016-151) – does not fit the research results. She posits instead that humans have cognitive gadgets – "special-purpose organs of thought" built in the course of development through social interaction. Similar criticisms are articulated by _Subrena E. Smith_ of the [University of New Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_Hampshire "University of New Hampshire").[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-152)
### Response by evolutionary psychologists
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=44 "Edit section: Response by evolutionary psychologists")]
Evolutionary psychologists have addressed many of their critics (e.g., in books by Segerstråle, Barlow, and Alcock.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTESegerstr%C3%A5le2000Barkow2006Alcock2001-153)). Among their rebuttals are that some criticisms are [straw men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straw_man "Straw man"), or are based on an incorrect nature versus nurture dichotomy, or on basic misunderstandings of the discipline.[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConferEastonFleischmanGoetz2010110%E2%80%9326Segerstr%C3%A5le2000ToobyBarrett2005305%E2%80%93337Hagan2005145%E2%80%93173Saad2009Geher2006181%E2%80%93202Johnson2011-154)
[Robert Kurzban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Kurzban "Robert Kurzban") suggested that _"...critics of the field, when they err, are not slightly missing the mark. Their confusion is profound. It's not like they are marksmen who can't quite hit the center of the target; they're holding the gun backwards."_ [[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzban2013-155) Many have written specifically to correct basic misconceptions.[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTECosmidesTooby1997Pinker1999119%E2%80%93127-156)[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-157)

## Notes
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=46 "Edit section: Notes")]
  1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_ref-31 "Jump up")** Quote from Encyclopædia Britannica's article -animal social behavior  
Despite this difficulty, there have been many careful and informative studies of human social behavior from an evolutionary perspective. Infanticide, intelligence, marriage patterns, promiscuity, perception of beauty, bride price, altruism, and the allocation of parental care have all been explored by testing predictions derived from the idea that conscious and unconscious behaviours have evolved to maximize inclusive fitness. The findings have been impressive."[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoenigDickinson2011-30)
  2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_ref-69 "Jump up")** The field of behavioral genetics is concerned with statistically partitioning differences between people into genetic and environmental sources of variance. However, understanding the concept of [heritability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heritability "Heritability") can be tricky – heritability refers only to differences between people, not to the degree to which individual traits are due to environmental or genetic factors, since traits are always a complex interweaving of both.
  3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_ref-79 "Jump up")** Workman & Reader page 277  
There are a number of hypotheses suggesting that language evolved to fulfill a social function such as social grooming (to bind large groups together), the making of social contracts (to enable monogamy and male provisioning) and the use of language to impress potential mates. While each of these hypotheses has its merits, each is still highly speculative and requires more evidence from different areas of research (such as linguistics and anthropology)."
  4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology#cite_ref-118 "Jump up")** Some individuals with bipolar disorder are especially creative during their manic phase, and the close relatives of people with schizophrenia have been found to be more likely to have creative professions.

### Academic societies
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=53 "Edit section: Academic societies")]
  * [Human Behavior and Evolution Society](http://www.hbes.com); international society dedicated to using evolutionary theory to study human nature
  * [The International Society for Human Ethology](https://web.archive.org/web/20060626003132/http://evolution.anthro.univie.ac.at/ishe/); promotes ethological perspectives on the study of humans worldwide
  * [European Human Behaviour and Evolution Association](http://www.ehbea.com/) an interdisciplinary society that supports the activities of European researchers with an interest in evolutionary accounts of human cognition, behavior and society
  * [The Association for Politics and the Life Sciences](http://www.aplsnet.org/); an international and interdisciplinary association of scholars, scientists, and policymakers concerned with evolutionary, genetic, and ecological knowledge and its bearing on political behavior, public policy and ethics.
  * [Society for Evolutionary Analysis in Law](http://www.sealsite.org/) a scholarly association dedicated to fostering interdisciplinary exploration of issues at the intersection of law, biology, and evolutionary theory
  * [The New England Institute for Cognitive Science and Evolutionary Psychology](https://web.archive.org/web/20070427064334/http://www.une.edu/nei/) aims to foster research and education into the interdisciplinary nexus of cognitive science and evolutionary studies
  * [The NorthEastern Evolutionary Psychology Society](http://www.neepsociety.com/); regional society dedicated to encouraging scholarship and dialogue on the topic of evolutionary psychology
  * [Feminist Evolutionary Psychology Society](https://web.archive.org/web/20110521023736/http://fepsociety.org/) researchers that investigate the active role that females have had in human evolution


### Journals
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Evolutionary_psychology&action=edit&section=54 "Edit section: Journals")]
  * _[Evolutionary Psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_Psychology_\(journal\) "Evolutionary Psychology \(journal\)")_ – free access online scientific journal
  * _[Evolution and Human Behavior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_and_Human_Behavior "Evolution and Human Behavior")_ – journal of the [Human Behavior and Evolution Society](http://www.hbes.com)
  * _Evolutionary Psychological Science_ - An international, interdisciplinary forum for original research papers that address evolved psychology. Spans social and life sciences, anthropology, philosophy, criminology, law and the humanities.
  * _[Politics and the Life Sciences](http://www.politicsandthelifesciences.org/)_ – an interdisciplinary peer-reviewed journal published by the [Association for Politics and the Life Sciences](http://www.aplsnet.org/)
  * _[Human Nature: An Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective](https://www.springer.com/social+sciences/anthropology+and+archaeology/journal/12110)_ – advances the interdisciplinary investigation of the biological, social, and environmental factors that underlie human behavior. It focuses primarily on the functional unity in which these factors are continuously and mutually interactive. These include the evolutionary, biological, and sociological processes as they interact with human social behavior.
  * _[Biological Theory: Integrating Development, Evolution and Cognition](https://web.archive.org/web/20070705175147/http://www.kli.ac.at/publications-a.html)_ – devoted to theoretical advances in the fields of biology and cognition, with an emphasis on the conceptual integration afforded by evolutionary and developmental approaches.
  * _[Evolutionary Anthropology](https://web.archive.org/web/20001210195300/http://www3.interscience.wiley.com/cgi-bin/jtoc?ID=38641)_
  * _[Behavioral and Brain Sciences](https://web.archive.org/web/20051221163333/http://www.bbsonline.org/)_ – interdisciplinary articles in psychology, neuroscience, behavioral biology, cognitive science, artificial intelligence, linguistics and philosophy. About 30% of the articles have focused on evolutionary analyses of behavior.
  * _[Evolution and Development](https://archive.today/20130105055909/http://www3.interscience.wiley.com/journal/118546131/home)_ – research relevant to interface of evolutionary and developmental biology
  * _[The Evolutionary Review – Art, Science, and Culture](http://www.evolutionaryreview.com/ed.htm)_
